# f.3.4  $v, \widetilde{v}$  move

the goal of this section is to prove lemma f.24.

lemma f.24  $(v, \widetilde{v}$  move). in the  $j$ -th iteration, we have,

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}) \geq \omega \big (\frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} \cdot k _ {j} \cdot g _ {k _ {j}}}{\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \log n} \big),
$$

in which  $\pi, \tau : [n] \to [n]$  are permutations such that  $y_{\pi(i)}^{(j)} \geq y_{\pi(i+1)}^{(j)}$  and  $x_{\tau(i)}^{(j+1)} \geq x_{\tau(i+1)}^{(j+1)}$ .

proof. case 1,  $k_{j} = 0$ . when  $k_{j} = 0$ , we didn't enter the matrixupdate procedure, so  $v^{(j + 1)} = v^{(j)}$ . if we further enter the partialmatrixupdate procedure and change  $\widetilde{v}$ , we have that  $\forall i\in [n]$

$$
y _ {i} ^ {(j)} - x _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} = \psi (w _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / \widetilde {v} _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1) - \psi (w _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / \widetilde {v} _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} - 1) \geq 0,
$$

where the first step follows by the definition of  $x_{i}^{(j + 1)}$  and  $y_{i}^{(j)}$  (definition f.15) and the fact that  $v$  do not change, and the last step follows by corollary f.14.

thus  $y_{i}^{(j)} \geq x_{i}^{(j + 1)}, \forall i \in [n]$ . since  $g_{i}$  and  $y_{\pi (i)}^{(j)}$  are both non-increasing, we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}) \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}) \geq 0 = \omega (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} \cdot k _ {j} \cdot g _ {k _ {j}} / (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \log n)),
$$

where the last step follows by  $k_{j} = 0$ .

case 2,  $k_{j} \neq 0$ . when  $k_{j} \neq 0$ , we must have entered matrixupdate, and by fact f.10, we must have  $k_{j} \geq n^{a}$ . by part 3 of fact f.12, the difference between  $x^{(j + 1)}$  and  $y^{(j)}$  is that in coordinates  $i \in \pi([k_j])$ ,  $x_i^{(j + 1)}$  is cleared to 0, and in other coordinates  $x_i^{(j + 1)}$  is the same with  $y_i^{(j)}$ . so we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}\right) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + k _ {j})} ^ {(j)}\right). \tag {62}
$$

note that when the subscripts are out of range, we define  $y_{\pi (n + 1)}^{(j)} = \dots = y_{\pi (n + k_j)}^{(j)} = 0$ .

part 2 of fact f.12 shows that

$$
y _ {\pi \left(k _ {j}\right)} ^ {(j)} \geq \epsilon_ {\text {f a r}} ^ {2} / \left(3 2 0 0 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right). \tag {63}
$$

part 1 of fact f.12 shows that either  $k_{j} = n$  or  $y_{\pi (k_j)}^{(j)} < (1 - 1 / \log n)y_{\pi (k_j / 1.5)}^{(j)}$ . if  $k_{j} = n$ , we let  $l = k_{j} = n$ , otherwise we let  $l = k_{j} / 1.5$ . the  $l$  we choose always satisfies that for all  $i \in [l]$ ,

$$
y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + k _ {j})} ^ {(j)} \geq y _ {\pi (l)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (1 + k _ {j})} ^ {(j)} \geq \epsilon_ {\text {f a r}} ^ {2} / \left(3 2 0 0 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \log n\right), \tag {64}
$$

where the first step follows by  $y_{\pi (i)}^{(j)}$  is non-increasing, the second step is true because:

1. in the case of  $k_{j} = n$ , we have  $y_{\pi (l)}^{(j)} = y_{\pi (k_j)}^{(j)} \geq \frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}^2}{3200\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}$  by eq. (63) and  $y_{\pi (k_j + 1)}^{(j)} = y_{\pi (n + 1)}^{(j)} = 0$ .  
2. in the case of  $y_{\pi(k_j)}^{(j)} < (1 - 1 / \log n) y_{\pi(k_j / 1.5)}^{(j)}$ , we have

$$
y _ {\pi (l)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (1 + k _ {j})} ^ {(j)} \geq y _ {\pi (k _ {j} / 1. 5)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (k _ {j})} ^ {(j)} \geq y _ {\pi (k _ {j} / 1. 5)} ^ {(j)} / \log n \geq \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} / (3 2 0 0 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \log n),
$$

where the second step follows from the inequality of  $y_{\pi(k_j)}^{(j)}$ , and the third step follows from eq. (63) and the fact that  $y_{\pi(i)}^{(j)}$  is non-increasing.

putting it all together, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}\right) \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + k _ {j})} ^ {(j)}\right) \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {l} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + k _ {j})} ^ {(j)}\right) \\ \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {l} g _ {i} \cdot \big (\frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2}}{3 2 0 0 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \log n} \big) = \omega \big (\frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} \cdot k _ {j} \cdot g _ {k _ {j}}}{\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \log n} \big), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first step is by eq. (62), the second step follows from  $y_{\pi(i)}^{(j)}$  is non-increasing and thus all terms  $\geq 0$ , the third step is by eq. (64), and the last step follows from  $g_l \geq g_{k_j}$  and  $l = \omega(k_j)$ .

# f.3.5  $\ell_2$ -norm of  $g$

lemma f.25 ( $\ell_2$ -norm of  $g$ ).  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$  (definition f.17) satisfies  $\| g \|_2 = o(n^{-a/2} + n^{\omega - 5/2})$ .

proof. for  $i \leq n^a$ , we have  $\sum_{i=1}^{n^a} g_i^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{n^a} n^{-2a} = n^{-a}$ .

for  $i > n^a$ , note that there exists  $i \in [n]$  such that  $i > n^a$  implies  $a < 1$ , so we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = n ^ {a} + 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} ^ {2} = \sum_ {i = n ^ {a} + 1} ^ {n} i ^ {\frac {2 (\omega - 2)}{1 - a} - 2} \cdot n ^ {- \frac {2 a (\omega - 2)}{1 - a}} = o (1) \cdot \int_ {n ^ {a + 1}} ^ {n} x ^ {\frac {2 (\omega - 2)}{1 - a} - 2} \cdot n ^ {- \frac {2 a (\omega - 2)}{1 - a}} d x \\ = o (1) \cdot \max \{n ^ {\frac {2 (\omega - 2)}{1 - a} - 1}, n ^ {\frac {2 a (\omega - 2)}{1 - a} - a} \} \cdot n ^ {- \frac {2 a (\omega - 2)}{1 - a}} = o (n ^ {2 \omega - 5} + n ^ {- a}). \\ \end{array}
$$

therefore, we have  $\| g\| _2 = o(n^{-a / 2} + n^{\omega -5 / 2})$

# f.4 amortized analysis for partialmatrixupdate

# f.4.1 definitions

definition f.26 ( $x$  and  $y$  for partialmatrixupdate). for any  $j \in \{0,1,\ldots,t-1\}$ , and for any  $i \in [n]$ , we define  $x_i^{(j)}$  and  $y_i^{(j)}$  as follows:

$$
x _ {i} ^ {(j)} := \psi (w _ {i} ^ {(j)} / \widetilde {v} _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1), \qquad y _ {i} ^ {(j)} := \psi (w _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / \widetilde {v} _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1),
$$

where  $v^{(j)}$ ,  $\widetilde{v}^{(j)}$  and  $w^{(j)}$  are defined as of definition f.1.

note that the difference between  $x_{i}^{(j)}$  and  $y_{i}^{(j)}$  is that  $w$  is changing. the difference between  $y_{i}^{(j)}$  and  $x_{i}^{(j + 1)}$  is that  $\widetilde{v}$  is changing.

definition f.27 (sorting permutations for partialmatrixupdate). for any  $j \in \{0,1,\ldots,t\}$ , let  $\tau_{j}$  be the permutation that  $x_{\tau_{j}(i)}^{(j)} \geq x_{\tau_{j}(i+1)}^{(j)}$ , and let  $\pi_{j}$  be the permutation that  $y_{\pi(i)}^{(j)} \geq y_{\pi(i+1)}^{(j)}$ .

when it is clear from the context that we are only arguing about the  $j$ -th iteration, for simplicity we assume the coordinates of vector  $x^{(j)} \in \mathbb{r}^n$  are sorted such that  $x_i^{(j)} \geq x_{i+1}^{(j)}$ . and we use  $\tau$  and  $\pi$  to denote the permutations such that  $x_{\tau(i)}^{(j+1)} \geq x_{\tau(i+1)}^{(j+1)}$  and  $y_{\pi(i)}^{(j)} \geq y_{\pi(i+1)}^{(j)}$ .

definition f.28 (g for partialmatrixupdate). for some  $\widetilde{a} \leq \alpha \cdot a$ , where  $\alpha$  is the dual exponent of matrix multiplication, and  $a$  is the parameter for matrixupdate, we define  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$  as follows:

$$
g _ {i} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} n ^ {- \widetilde {a}}, & \text {i f} i \leq n ^ {\widetilde {a}}; \\ i ^ {\frac {a (\omega - 2)}{a - \widetilde {a}} - 1} \cdot n ^ {- \frac {a \widetilde {a} (\omega - 2)}{a - \widetilde {a}}}, & \text {i f} i \in (n ^ {\widetilde {a}}, n ^ {a} ]; \\ 0 & \text {i f} i > n ^ {a}. \end{array} \right.
$$

note that  $g$  is non-increasing. for all  $\widetilde{k}_j \in (n^{\widetilde{a}}, n^a]$ ,  $(\widetilde{k}_j \cdot g_{\widetilde{k}_j} n^{2a}) = \widetilde{k}_j^{\frac{a(\omega - 2)}{a - \widetilde{a}}} \cdot n^{2a - \frac{a\widetilde{a}(\omega - 2)}{a - \widetilde{a}}}$  is an upper bound of  $\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n^a, n^a, \widetilde{k}_j)$  of multiplying a  $n^a \times n^a$  matrix with a  $n^a \times \widetilde{k}_j$  matrix. for more details please refer to [gu18].

definition f.29 (potential function  $\phi$  for partialmatrixupdate). we define the potential function in the  $j$ -th iteration as

$$
\phi_ {j} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot x _ {\tau_ {j} (i)} ^ {(j)}.
$$

note that we always have  $\phi_j \geq 0$  since  $\forall i$ ,  $g_i$  and  $x_i^{(j)}$  are both non-negative.

# f.4.2 main result

lemma f.30 (amortized time for partialmatrixupdate). let sequences  $\{w^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$ ,  $\{v^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$ ,  $\{\widetilde{v}^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$  be defined as of definition f.1, let  $\widetilde{k}_j$  be defined as of definition f.3, and let  $\{x^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$ ,  $\{y^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$ ,  $g$ ,  $\phi$  be defined as of definition f.15, f.28 and f.29. if we further have the condition that the input sequence satisfies the following:  $\forall j \in \{0, \dots, t - 1\}$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\mathbb {e} [ w _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} | w ^ {(j)} ] / w _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1) ^ {2} \leq c _ {1} ^ {2}, \quad \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\mathbb {e} [ (w _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / w _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1) ^ {2} \mid w ^ {(j)} ]) ^ {2} \leq c _ {2} ^ {2}, \quad | w _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / w _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1 | \leq 1 / 4.
$$

then, we have that in expectation

$$
\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} \widetilde {k} _ {j} g _ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} = o \left(\left(c _ {1} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot \log n \cdot \| g \| _ {2}\right).
$$

further, combining with lemma e.18, the expected amortized running time per iteration of partialmatrixupdate is

$$
o \big ((c _ {1} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \cdot (n ^ {1 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2} + n ^ {1 + (\omega - 3 / 2) a}) \log n \big).
$$

proof. similar to the proof of lemma f.19, we upper bound how much the potential function can increase due to changing  $w^{(j)}$  to  $w^{(j + 1)}$  (in section f.4.3), and also lower bound how much the potential function can decrease because of changing  $\widetilde{v}^{(j)}$  to  $\widetilde{v}^{(j + 1)}$  (in section f.4.4).

similar to lemma f.19, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} 0 \leq \mathbb {e} [ \phi_ {t} ] - \phi_ {0} = \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {t - 1} \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \underbrace {\mathbb {e} \left[ y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {i} ^ {(j)} \right]} _ {w \mathrm {m o v e}} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \underbrace {\mathbb {e} \left[ y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)} \right]} _ {\widetilde {v} \mathrm {m o v e}}\right) \\ \leq \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {t - 1} \left(o (c _ {1} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}) \cdot \| g \| _ {2} - \omega (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \widetilde {k} _ {j} g _ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} / \log n)\right) \\ = t \cdot o \left(c _ {1} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \| g \| _ {2} - \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} \omega \left(\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \widetilde {k} _ {j} g _ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} / \log n\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the third step follows from lemma f.31 which states that  $\forall w^{(j)}, v^{(j)}, \widetilde{v}^{(j)}$ , we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \mathbb {e} \left[ y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {i} ^ {(j)} \mid w ^ {(j)}, v ^ {(j)}, \widetilde {v} ^ {(j)} \right] \leq o (c _ {1} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}) \cdot \| g \| _ {2},
$$

then this upper bound also holds for unconditional expectation, the third step also follows from lemma f.33 which states that  $\sum_{i=1}^{n} g_i \cdot \left(y_{\pi(i)}^{(j)} - x_{\tau(i)}^{(j+1)}\right) \geq \omega(\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} \widetilde{k}_j g_{\widetilde{k}_j} / \log n)$ .

therefore, we have

$$
\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} \widetilde {k} _ {j} g _ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} = o \left(\left(c _ {1} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot \log n \cdot \| g \| _ {2}\right).
$$

using lemma e.18, we have that the expected amortized running time per iteration of partialmatrixupdate is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} \mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (n, n ^ {a}, \widetilde {k} _ {j}) \leq \frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} n ^ {1 - a} \cdot \mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} (n ^ {a}, n ^ {a}, \widetilde {k} _ {j}) \leq \frac {n ^ {1 + a}}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} \widetilde {k} _ {j} g _ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} \\ = o \left(\left(c _ {1} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot n ^ {1 + a} \log n \cdot \| g \| _ {2}\right) \\ = o \big ((c _ {1} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \cdot (n ^ {1 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2} + n ^ {1 + (\omega - 3 / 2) a}) \log n \big), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first step follows from the fact that we can always divide a  $n \times n^a$  matrix into  $n^{1 - a}$  copies of  $n^a \times n^a$  matrices, the second step follows from definition f.28 of  $g$  which gives that

$\mathcal{t}_{\mathrm{mat}}(n^a, n^a, \widetilde{k}_j) \leq n^{2a} \cdot \widetilde{k}_j g_{\widetilde{k}_j}$  for  $n^{\widetilde{a}} \leq \widetilde{k}_j \leq n^a$ , and we indeed have  $\widetilde{k}_j \geq n^{\widetilde{a}}$  (fact f.11) and  $\widetilde{k}_j \leq 2n^a$  (lemma e.1) when entering partialmatrixupdate. (we ignore the 2 factor since it can only increase the final amortized time by a constant factor.) the fourth step follows from lemma f.34 that  $\|g\|_2 = o(n^{-\widetilde{a}/2} + n^{a\omega - 5a/2})$ .

# f.4.3  $w$  move

the goal of this section is to prove lemma f.31.

lemma f.31 (w move). in the  $j$ -th iteration, for any possible values  $w^{(j)}$ ,  $v^{(j)}$ , and  $\widetilde{v}^{(j)}$ , we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \mathbb {e} \left[ y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {i} ^ {(j)} \mid w ^ {(j)}, v ^ {(j)}, \widetilde {v} ^ {(j)} \right] \leq o \left(c _ {1} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot \| g \| _ {2}. \tag {65}
$$

proof. for simplicity, in this proof we write  $\mathbb{e}[\cdot ]$  as a shorthand of  $\mathbb{e}[\cdot |w^{(j)},v^{(j)},\widetilde{v}^{(j)}]$

similar to the proof of lemma f.20, we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \mathbb {e} \left[ y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {i} ^ {(j)} \right] \leq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \mathbb {e} \left[ y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} \right] = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \mathbb {e} \left[ \psi \left(w _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j + 1)} / \widetilde {v} _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - 1\right) - \psi \left(w _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} / \widetilde {v} _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - 1\right) \right]
$$

where the first step follows from that the non-negative values  $x_{i}^{(j)}$  are sorted in descending order, and  $g$  is also non-increasing, the second step follows from the definitions of  $x^{(j)}$  and  $y^{(j)}$  (definition f.26).

now  $\sum_{i=1}^{n} g_i \cdot \mathbb{e}[y_{\pi(i)}^{(j)} - x_i^{(j)}] \leq o(c_1 + c_2 / \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}) \|g\|_2$  directly follows from lemma f.32.

it remains to prove the following lemma.

lemma f.32. in the  $j$ -th iteration, for any  $w^{(j)}$ ,  $v^{(j)}$ , and  $\widetilde{v}^{(j)}$  we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \mathbb {e} \left[ \psi \left(w _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j + 1)} / \widetilde {v} _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - 1\right) - \psi \left(w _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} / \widetilde {v} _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - 1\right) \mid w ^ {(j)}, v ^ {(j)}, \widetilde {v} ^ {(j)} \right] = o \left(c _ {1} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot \| g \| _ {2}.
$$

proof. the proof of this lemma is exactly the same as that of lemma f.21, just replace all  $v$  with  $\widetilde{v}$  in the proof of lemma f.21.

# f.4.4  $\widetilde{v}$  move

the goal of this section is to prove lemma f.33.

lemma f.33 ( $\widetilde{v}$  move). in the  $j$ -th iteration, we have,

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}\right) \geq \omega \left(\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \widetilde {k} _ {j} g _ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} / \log n\right).
$$

proof. case 1. if we enter the matrixupdate procedure, we have  $\widetilde{k}_j = 0$  since we won't enter the else branch in line 13 of updatev (algorithm 9). from part 3 of fact f.12, we know that  $\forall i \leq k_j$ ,  $\widetilde{v}_{\pi(i)}^{(j+1)} = w_{\pi(i)}^{(j+1)}$ , and  $\forall i > k_j$ ,  $\widetilde{v}_{\pi(i)}^{(j+1)} = \widetilde{v}_{\pi(i)}^{(j)}$ . therefore,  $\forall i \in [n]$

$$
x _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} = \psi \left(w _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / \widetilde {v} _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} - 1\right) \leq \psi \left(w _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / \widetilde {v} _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1\right) = y _ {i} ^ {(j)},
$$

where the first and the third steps follow by the definition of  $x^{(j + 1)}$  and  $y^{(j)}$  (definition f.26). this means  $y_{i}^{(j)}\geq x_{i}^{(j + 1)},\forall i\in [n]$ . since  $g_{i}$  and  $y_{\pi (i)}^{(j)}$  are both non-increasing, we have

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}) \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}) \geq 0 = \omega (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \widetilde {k _ {j}} g _ {\widetilde {k _ {j}}} / \log n),
$$

where the last step follows by  $\widetilde{k}_j = 0$ .

case 2. if we do not enter both the matrixupdate and the partialmatrixupdate procedure, nothing happens and  $x^{(j + 1)}$  is the same as  $y^{(j)}$ , this statement also holds.

case 3. now we only need to consider the case where we enter the partialmatrixupdate procedure. by part 3 of fact f.13,  $x^{(j + 1)}$  satisfies that in coordinates  $i \in \pi([\widetilde{k}_j])$ ,  $x_i^{(j + 1)} \leq \frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}{200\log n}$ , and in coordinates  $i \notin \pi([\widetilde{k}_j])$ ,  $x^{(j + 1)}$  is the same with  $y^{(j)}$ . so we decompose  $x^{(j + 1)}$  into two pieces  $x^{(j + 1)} = x_1 + x_2$ , where  $x_1$  copies the values on coordinates  $i \in \pi([\widetilde{k}_j])$  and has 0 on other coordinates, and  $x_2$  copies the values on coordinates  $i \notin \pi([\widetilde{k}_j])$  and has 0 on other coordinates. and when the subscripts are out of range, we define  $y_{\pi(n + 1)}^{(j)} = \dots = y_{\pi(n + k_j)}^{(j)} = 0$ . we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {1, \tau (i)} - x _ {2, \tau (i)}) \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {2, \tau (i)}) - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} g _ {i} \cdot \frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}}{2 0 0 \log n} \\ = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot (y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + \widetilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)}) - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} g _ {i} \cdot \frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}}{2 0 0 \log n} \\ \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + \widetilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)}\right) - 1. 5 \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\widetilde {k} _ {j} / 1. 5} g _ {i} \cdot \frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}}{2 0 0 \log n}, \tag {66} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the second step follows by  $x_{1\tau(i)} \leq \frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}}{200\log n}$  for  $\tau(i) \in \pi([\widetilde{k}_j])$  and  $x_{1\tau(i)} = 0$  for  $\tau(i) \notin \pi([\widetilde{k}_j])$ , the third step follows by  $x_{2,\tau(i)} = 0$  for  $\tau(i) \in \pi([\widetilde{k}_j])$  and  $x_{2,\tau(i)} = y_{\tau(i)}^{(j)}$  for  $i \notin \pi([\widetilde{k}_j])$ , and the last step follows by  $g$  is non-increasing.

part 2 of fact f.13 shows that

$$
y _ {\pi \left(\tilde {k} _ {j}\right)} ^ {(j)} \geq \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / 1 0 0 \tag {67}
$$

part 1 of fact f.13 shows that either  $\widetilde{k}_j = n$  or  $y_{\pi (\widetilde{k}_j)}^{(j)} < (1 - 1 / \log n)\cdot y_{\pi (\widetilde{k}_j / 1.5)}^{(j)}$ . if  $\widetilde{k}_j = n$ , we let  $l = \widetilde{k}_j = n$ , otherwise we let  $l = \widetilde{k}_j / 1.5$ . the  $l$  we choose always satisfies that for all  $i\in [l]$

$$
y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + \tilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)} \geq y _ {\pi (l)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (1 + \tilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)} \geq \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / (1 0 0 \log n), \tag {68}
$$

where the first step follows by  $y_{\pi (i)}^{(j)}$  is non-increasing, the second step is true because:

1. in the case of  $\widetilde{k}_j = n$ , we have  $y_{\pi(l)}^{(j)} = y_{\pi(\widetilde{k}_j)}^{(j)} \geq \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} / 100$  by eq. (67) and  $y_{\pi(\widetilde{k}_j + 1)}^{(j)} = y_{\pi(n + 1)}^{(j)} = 0$ .  
2. in the case of  $y_{\pi (\widetilde{k}_j)}^{(j)} < (1 - 1 / \log n)\cdot y_{\pi (\widetilde{k}_j / 1.5)}^{(j)}$ , we have

$$
y _ {\pi (l)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (1 + \widetilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)} \geq y _ {\pi (\widetilde {k} _ {j} / 1. 5)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (\widetilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)} \geq y _ {\pi (\widetilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)} / \log n \geq \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / (1 0 0 \log n),
$$

where the second step follows from the inequality of  $y_{\pi (\widetilde{k}_j)}^{(j)}$ , and the third step follows from eq. (67).

putting it all together, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - x _ {\tau (i)} ^ {(j + 1)}\right) \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + \widetilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)}\right) - 1. 5 \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\widetilde {k} _ {j} / 1. 5} g _ {i} \cdot \frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}}{2 0 0 \log n} \\ \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {l} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + \widetilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)}\right) - 1. 5 \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\widetilde {k} _ {j} / 1. 5} g _ {i} \cdot \frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}}{2 0 0 \log n} \\ \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {l} g _ {i} \cdot \left(y _ {\pi (i)} ^ {(j)} - y _ {\pi (i + \widetilde {k} _ {j})} ^ {(j)} - 1. 5 \frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}}{2 0 0 \log n}\right) \\ \geq \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {l} g _ {i} \cdot (\frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}}{1 0 0 \log n} - 1. 5 \frac {\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}}{2 0 0 \log n}) = \omega (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} \cdot \widetilde {k} _ {j} \cdot g _ {\widetilde {k} _ {j}} / \log n). \\ \end{array}
$$

where first step is by eq. (66), the second step follows from  $y_{\pi(i)}^{(j)}$  is non-increasing and thus all terms  $\geq 0$ , the third step follows from  $l \geq \widetilde{k}_j / 1.5$ , the forth step is by eq. (68), and the last step follows from  $g_l \geq g_{\widetilde{k}_j}$  and  $l = \omega(\widetilde{k}_j)$ .

# f.4.5  $\ell_2$ -norm of  $g$

lemma f.34.  $g\in \mathbb{r}^n$  (definition f.28) satisfies  $\| g\| _2 = o(n^{-\tilde{a} /2} + n^{a\omega -5a / 2})$

proof. the proof is same as that of lemma f.25. we use  $n^a$  to replace  $n$ , and  $n^{\widetilde{a}}$  to replace  $n^a$ .

# f.5 amortized analysis for vectorupdate

lemma f.35 (amortized time for vectorupdate). let sequences  $\{h^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$ ,  $\{g^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$ ,  $\{\widetilde{g}^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$  be defined as of definition f.2, and let  $p_j$  be defined as of definition f.3. if we further have the condition that the input sequence satisfies the following:  $\forall j \in \{0, \dots, t - 1\}$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\mathbb {e} [ h _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} | h ^ {(j)} ] / h _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1) ^ {2} \leq c _ {4} ^ {2}, \quad \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\mathbb {e} [ (h _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / h _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1) ^ {2} | h ^ {(j)} ]) ^ {2} \leq c _ {5} ^ {2}, \quad | h _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / h _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1 | \leq 1 / 4.
$$

then, we have that in expectation

1.  $\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j = 1}^{t}p_{j}n^{1 + o(1)} = o\big((c_{4}\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} / \epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}^{2} + c_{5} / \epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}^{2})\cdot \log n\cdot n^{1.5 + o(1)}\big),$  
2.  $\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j = 1}^{t}n^{2a}\cdot \mathbf{1}_{p_j > 0} = o\bigl ((c_4\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} / \epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}^2 +c_5 / \epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}^2)\cdot \log n\cdot n^{1.5a}\bigr).$

further, combining with lemma e.27, the expected amortized running time per iteration of vectorupdate is

$$
o \big ((c _ {4} \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2}) \cdot \log n \cdot n ^ {1. 5 + o (1)} \big).
$$

proof. part 1. for the first equation, we define  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$  to be  $g_i = 1$ ,  $\forall i \in [n]$ . note that  $g$  is non-increasing,  $n^{1 + o(1)} \cdot (p_j g_{p_j}) = p_j n^{1 + o(1)}$ , and  $\| g \|_2 = \sqrt{n}$ . then we can use the same argument as lemma f.19 for matrixupdate to prove that

$$
\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} p _ {j} n ^ {1 + o (1)} = o \left(\left(c _ {4} \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot \log n \cdot n ^ {1 + o (1)} \cdot \| g \| _ {2}\right)
$$

$$
= o \big ((c _ {4} \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2}) \cdot \log n \cdot n ^ {1. 5 + o (1)} \big).
$$

part 2. for the second equation, we define  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$  to be

$$
g _ {i} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} n ^ {- a}, & i \leq n ^ {a}, \\ i ^ {- 1}, & n ^ {a} <   i <   n. \end{array} \right.
$$

note that  $g$  is non-increasing, and  $n^{2a} \cdot \mathbf{1}_{p_j > 0} = n^{2a} \cdot \mathbf{1}_{p_j \geq n^a} \leq n^{2a} \cdot (p_j g_{p_j})$  since analogous to fact f.10 we have that either  $p_j = 0$  or  $p_j \geq n^a$ . we also have

$$
\| g \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {n ^ {a}}{n ^ {2 a}} + \int_ {n ^ {a}} ^ {n} x ^ {- 2} \mathrm {d} x = n ^ {- a} + n ^ {- a} - n ^ {- 1} = o (n ^ {- a}).
$$

then we can use the same argument as lemma f.19 for matrixupdate to prove that

$$
\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} n ^ {2 a} \cdot \mathbf {1} _ {p _ {j} > 0} = o \big ((c _ {4} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \log n \cdot n ^ {2 a} \| g \| _ {2} \big) = o \big ((c _ {4} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \cdot \log n \cdot n ^ {1. 5 a} \big).
$$

combine part 1 and part 2. using lemma e.27 and note that  $n^{1.5a} \leq n^{1.5 + o(1)}$ , we have that the expected amortized running time per iteration of vectorupdate is

$$
\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} (p _ {j} n ^ {1 + o (1)} + n ^ {2 a} \mathbf {1} _ {p _ {j} > 0}) = o \big ((c _ {4} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \cdot \log n \cdot n ^ {1. 5 + o (1)} \big).
$$

![](images/0f537b6d2f50909597a2ff6806ac2a4c3183eb54e797f6441046d8ad54091d91.jpg)

# f.6 amortized analysis for partial vector update

lemma f.36 (amortized time for partial vector update). let sequences  $\{h^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$ ,  $\{g^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$ ,  $\{\widetilde{g}^{(j)}\}_{j = 0}^{t}$  be defined as of definition f.2, and let  $\widetilde{p}_j$  be defined as of definition f.3. if we further have the condition that the input sequence satisfies the following:  $\forall j \in \{0, \dots, t - 1\}$

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\mathbb {e} [ h _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} | h ^ {(j)} ] / h _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1) ^ {2} \leq c _ {4} ^ {2}, \quad \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (\mathbb {e} [ (h _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / h _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1) ^ {2} \mid h ^ {(j)} ]) ^ {2} \leq c _ {5} ^ {2}, \quad | h _ {i} ^ {(j + 1)} / h _ {i} ^ {(j)} - 1 | \leq 1 / 4.
$$

then, we have that in expectation

1.  $\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j = 1}^{t}\widetilde{p}_jn^{1 + o(1)} = o\big((c_4 / \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} + c_5 / \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}^2)\cdot \log n\cdot n^{1.5 + o(1)}\big),$  
2.  $\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j = 1}^{t}n^{2a}\cdot \mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{p}_j > 0} = o\big((c_4 / \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} + c_5 / \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}^2)\cdot \log n\cdot n^{2a - \widetilde{a} /2}\big).$

further, combining with lemma e.33, the expected amortized running time per iteration of partial vector update is

$$
o \big ((c _ {4} \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2}) \cdot \log n \cdot (n ^ {1. 5 + o (1)} + n ^ {2 a - \widetilde {a} / 2}) \big).
$$

proof. first note that we always have  $\widetilde{p}_j\leq 2n^a$  by lemma e.1.

part 1. for the first equation, we define  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$  to be  $g_i = 1$ ,  $\forall i \in [2n^a]$ , and  $g_i = 0$ ,  $\forall i \notin [2n^a]$ . note that  $g$  is non-increasing,  $n^{a + o(1)} \cdot (\widetilde{p}_j g_{\widetilde{p}_j}) = \widetilde{p}_j n^{a + o(1)}$ , and  $\| g \|_2 = \sqrt{2n^a}$ . then we can use the same argument as lemma f.30 for partialmatrixupdate to prove that

$$
\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} \widetilde {p} _ {j} n ^ {a + o (1)} = o \big ((c _ {4} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \log n \cdot n ^ {a + o (1)} \cdot \| g \| _ {2} \big) = o \big ((c _ {4} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \cdot \log n \cdot n ^ {1. 5 a + o (1)} \big).
$$

![](images/48dedad0b87057809a576c641abf7541f850a31fb546c0e9526d5980bae22480.jpg)  
figure 9:  $\psi (x),\psi (x)^{\prime}$  and  $\psi (x)^{\prime \prime}$ . for  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}\in (0,1)$ , and for simplicity we use  $\epsilon$  in the figures.

![](images/1ba37ba607d640203c0f19f779a54dd407e5b9a26e81f48f23bf125360ef84ee.jpg)

![](images/d4a5bfd7df24a7d4a6c02a3c4e3dace7351b13584bdf25f404d9b04131da217f.jpg)

part 2. for the second equation, we let  $g \in \mathbb{r}^n$  be

$$
g _ {i} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} n ^ {- \widetilde {a}}, & i \leq n ^ {\widetilde {a}}, \\ i ^ {- 1}, & n ^ {\widetilde {a}} <   i \leq 2 n ^ {a}, \\ 0, & i > 2 n ^ {a}. \end{array} \right.
$$

note that  $g$  is non-increasing, and  $n^{2a} \cdot \mathbf{1}_{p_j > 0} = n^{2a} \cdot \mathbf{1}_{p_j \geq n^{\widetilde{a}}} \leq n^{2a} \cdot (p_j g_{p_j})$  since analogous to fact f.11 we have that either  $\widetilde{p}_j = 0$  or  $\widetilde{p}_j \geq n^{\widetilde{a}}$ . we also have

$$
\| g \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \frac {n ^ {\widetilde {a}}}{n ^ {2 \widetilde {a}}} + \int_ {n ^ {\widetilde {a}}} ^ {2 n ^ {a}} x ^ {- 2} \mathrm {d} x = n ^ {- \widetilde {a}} + n ^ {- \widetilde {a}} - n ^ {- a} / 2 = o (n ^ {- \widetilde {a}}).
$$

then we can use the same argument as the lemma f.30 for partialmatrixupdate to prove that

$$
\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} n ^ {2 a} \cdot {\bf 1} _ {\widetilde {p} _ {j} > 0} = o \big ((c _ {4} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \log n \cdot n ^ {2 a} \| g \| _ {2} \big) = o \big ((c _ {4} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \log n \cdot n ^ {2 a - \widetilde {a} / 2} \big).
$$

combine part 1 and part 2. using lemma e.33 and note that  $n^{1.5a} \leq n^{1.5}$ , we have that the expected amortized running time per iteration of partial vectorupdate is

$$
\frac {1}{t} \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {t} (p _ {j} n ^ {1 + o (1)} + n ^ {2 a} \cdot {\bf 1} _ {p _ {j} > 0}) = o \big ((c _ {4} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}) \cdot \log n \cdot (n ^ {1. 5 + o (1)} + n ^ {2 a - \widetilde {a} / 2}) \big).
$$

# f.7 potential function  $\psi$

lemma f.37 (properties of function  $\psi$ , lemma 5.10 of [cls19]). let function  $\psi$  be defined as of definition f.4. then function  $\psi$  satisfies the following properties:

1. symmetric:  $\psi (-x) = \psi (x)$  and  $\psi (0) = 0$  
2.  $\psi(|x|)$  is non-decreasing;  
3.  $|\psi'(x)| = \omega(1), \forall |x| \leq 1.5\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}$ ;  
4.  $l_{1} \coloneqq \max_{x} \psi'(x) = 1$  and  $l_{2} \coloneqq \max_{x} \psi''(x) = 1 / \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}$ ;  
5.  $\psi (x)$  is a constant for  $|x|\geq 2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}$

proof. we can see that

$$
\psi (x) ^ {\prime} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {| x |}{\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}} & | x | \in [ 0, \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ] \\ \frac {2 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} - | x |}{\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}} & | x | \in (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}, 2 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ] \\ 0 & x \in (2 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}, + \infty) \end{array} \right. \quad \mathrm {a n d} \quad \psi (x) ^ {\prime \prime} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {1}{\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}} & x \in [ 0, \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ] \cup [ - 2 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}, - \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ] \\ - \frac {1}{\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}} & x \in (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}, 2 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ] \cup [ - \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}, 0 ] \\ 0 & x \in (2 \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}, + \infty) \end{array} \right.
$$

from the  $\psi (x)^{\prime}$  and  $\psi (x)^{\prime \prime}$ , it is not hard to see that  $\psi$  satisfies the properties needed.

algorithm 17 main algorithm  
1: procedure main(a,b,c,δ,a,tilde) ▷ theorem g.3   
2: ▷ a,b,c are inputs of lp   
3: ▷  $\delta$  is the accuracy parameter   
4:  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}\gets 10^{-5} / \log n,\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}\gets \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} / 100\log n,\epsilon \gets 10^{-7} / \log n$    
5:  $\lambda \leftarrow 40\log n$ $b_{\mathrm{sketch}}\gets 10^{12}\sqrt{n}\log^8 n / \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}^2$ $l_{\mathrm{sketch}}\gets n^{1 / 2 + o(1)}$    
6:  $\delta \leftarrow \min \{\frac{\delta}{2},\frac{1}{\lambda}\}$    
7: create lsketch sketching matrices  $r_{1},r_{2},\dots ,r_{l_{\mathrm{sketch}}}\in \mathbb{r}^{b_{\mathrm{sketch}}\times n}$  ▷ lemma b.14   
8: let  $r = [r_1^\top ,r_2^\top ,\dots ,r_{l_{\mathrm{sketch}}}^\top ]^\top$    
9: modify the linear program and obtain an initial  $x$  and  $s$    
10: let  $\mathrm{mp}_t$ $\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}$  be projection maintenance data structures.   
11: let  $f_{t}:x\mapsto \sqrt{x}$  , and  $f_{\phi}:x\mapsto \lambda \sinh (\lambda (x - 1)) / \sqrt{x}$    
12: t-1   
13: mp.t.initiaze(f,t,εmp,εfar,a,tilde,bsketch,lsketch,a,x,s,xs,r) ▷ algorithm 5   
14: mp. initialize(f,εmp,εfar,a,tilde,bsketch,lsketch,a,x,s,xt,r) ▷ algorithm 5   
15: while  $t > \delta^2 /(32n^3)$  do   
16:  $t^{\mathrm{new}}\gets (1 - \frac{\epsilon}{3\sqrt{n}})t$    
17: repeat   
18:  $\delta_x,\delta_s\gets \mathrm{onestepcentralpath}(\mathrm{mp}_t,\mathrm{mp}_\phi ,x,s,t,t^{\mathrm{new}})$  ▷ algorithm 3   
19: if the lsketch sketching matrices are used up then   
20: re-initialize  $\mathrm{mp}_t$  and  $\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}$  with new sketching matrices.   
21: end if   
22: until  $\| x^{-1}\delta_x\|_\infty \leq 3\epsilon$ $\| s^{-1}\delta_s\|_\infty \leq 3\epsilon$    
23:  $x^{\mathrm{new}}\gets x + \delta_x$ $s^{\mathrm{new}}\gets s + \delta_s$    
24: if  $\phi_{\lambda}(x^{\mathrm{new}}s^{\mathrm{new}} / t - 1) > n^{3}$  then   
25:  $(x^{\mathrm{new}},s^{\mathrm{new}})\gets \mathrm{classicalstep}(x,s,t^{\mathrm{new}})\quad \triangleright$  use the central path step of [vai89].   
26: construct sketching matrices  $r$  similar as before.   
27: mp.t.initiaze(f1,εmp,εfar,a,tilde,bsketch,lsketch,a,xnew,snew,xnew,snew,r) ▷ algorithm 5   
28: mp. initialize(f2,εmp,εfar,a,tilde,bsketch,lsketch,a,xnew,snew,xnew,snew,t,r) ▷ algorithm 5   
29: end if   
30:  $x\gets x^{\mathrm{new}},s\gets s^{\mathrm{new}},t\gets t^{\mathrm{new}}$    
31: end while   
32: return an approximate solution of the original linear program   
33: end procedure

# g combining data structure with optimization

in this section we combine the results of optimization and data structure to prove theorem 4.1.

lemma g.1. during the main algorithm (algorithm 17), we have the following guarantees:

1. assumption b.1, b.26, and f.5 about the error parameters are always satisfied.  
2. assumption b.5 that  $\widetilde{\mu} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} \overline{\mu}$ ,  $\widetilde{w} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} \overline{w}$ , and  $\overline{\mu} \approx_{0.1} t$  is always satisfied.  
3. the classicalstep (line 25 of algorithm 17) is executed with probability at most  $\frac{10}{n^2}$  in each iteration.  
4. in expectation the repeat-loop on line 17 of algorithm 17 is executed at most 2 times.

<table><tr><td>notation</td><td>ε</td><td>εmp</td><td>εfar</td><td>λ</td><td>b</td></tr><tr><td>choice</td><td>10-7/log n</td><td>10-5/log n</td><td>10-7/log2n</td><td>40 log n</td><td>1022√n log10n</td></tr></table>

table 15: extension of table 7. summary of choice of  $\epsilon$ ,  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}$ ,  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}$ ,  $\lambda$  and  $b$ . assigned in main procedure (algorithm 17). they are used to prove theorem g.3.

proof. part 1. after plugging in the parameters in table 15, it is straightforward to see that the constraints stated in assumption b.1, b.26, and f.5 are all satisfied.

part 2. the assumption that  $\widetilde{\mu} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} \overline{\mu}$  follows from part (b) of the correctness of updatequery in theorem c.9 that  $h^{\mathrm{appr}} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} h^{\mathrm{new}}$ , and the assumption that  $\widetilde{w} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} \overline{w}$  follows from part (a) of the correctness of updatequery in theorem c.9 that  $w^{\mathrm{appr}} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} w^{\mathrm{new}}$ .

finally, whenever  $\phi_{\lambda}(xs / t - 1) > n^3$ , the main algorithm runs the procedure classicalstep, and the  $(x,s)$  returned by classicalstep is guaranteed to satisfy  $xs \approx_{0.01} t$  (see [vai89]). also, if  $\phi_{\lambda}(xs / t - 1) \leq n^3$ , we have that  $e^{\lambda |x_i s_i / t - 1|} \leq n^3$ , and since  $\lambda \geq 30 \log n$  (part 2 of assumption b.26), we have  $|x_i s_i / t - 1| \leq 0.1$ . thus  $\overline{\mu} \approx_{0.1} t$  is always satisfied as well.

**part 3.** let  $\phi^{(i)} = \phi_{\lambda}(\frac{x^{(i)}s^{(i)}}{t^{(i)}} -1)$  denote the value of the potential function in the  $i$ -th iteration. we use induction to prove  $\mathbb{e}[\phi^{(i)}]\leq 10n$ , for all  $i$ . in the beginning of the main algorithm,  $x_{i}s_{i} = 1 = t$ ,  $\forall i\leq n$ . therefore in the base case,  $\phi^{(0)} = n < 10n$ . if the algorithm executes classicalstep in the  $i$ -th iteration, classicalstep outputs  $x$  and  $s$  that  $xs\approx_{0.01}t$ , and since  $\lambda \leq 60\log n$  (part 7 of assumption b.26),  $\phi^{(i)}\leq 10n$ . if the algorithm doesn't execute classicalstep, lemma b.27 gives us  $\mathbb{e}[\phi^{(i)}]\leq (1 - \frac{\lambda\epsilon}{15\sqrt{n}})\mathbb{e}[\phi^{(i - 1)}] + \frac{\lambda\epsilon}{15\sqrt{n}} 10n$ . therefore  $\mathbb{e}[\phi^{(i)}]\leq 10n$  since we have  $\mathbb{e}[\phi^{(i - 1)}]\leq 10n$  from induction hypothesis. then using markov's inequality we have  $\operatorname*{pr}[\phi^{(k)} > n^3 ]\leq 10 / n^2$ . thus the classicalstep on line 25 of algorithm 17 is executed with probability at most  $10 / n^2$  in each iteration.

part 4. from part 4 of lemma b.16 we have that  $\| x^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\infty} > 3\epsilon$  and  $\| s^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\infty} > 3\epsilon$  each happens with probability at most  $1 / n^4$ . thus in expectation the repeat-loop on line 17 of algorithm 17 is executed at most 2 times.

lemma g.2. for  $\epsilon \in (0,1 / 10000)$ ,  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} \in (0,1 / 10000)$ , and  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}} = \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} / 100\log n$ , each iteration of main (algorithm 17) takes

$$
o ^ {*} \big ((\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}) \cdot (n ^ {\omega - 1 / 2} + n ^ {2 - a / 2} + n ^ {1 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2}) + n ^ {(\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a} + n ^ {1 + b} \big)
$$

expected amortized time per iteration, where  $\omega$  is the exponent of matrix multiplication,  $\alpha$  is the dual exponent of matrix multiplication,  $0 \leq a \leq \alpha$  and  $0 \leq \widetilde{a} \leq a\alpha$  are the thresholds used by the data structure, and  $n^b$  is the sketching size.  $o^*$  notation hides all  $n^{o(1)}$  terms.

proof. part 3 of lemma g.1 shows that in each iteration classicalstep is executed with probability at most  $o(1/n^2)$ . since the cost of classicalstep is  $o(n^{2.5})$ , the amortized cost of executing classicalstep is  $o(n^{0.5})$  for one iteration.

part 4 of lemma g.1 shows that in expectation onestepcentralpath is executed at most 2 times in each iteration. so now we only need to bound the running time of the procedure onestepcentralpath (algorithm 3). in the procedure onestepcentralpath, we call the procedure updatequery of the data structures (algorithm 8) two times. since the time analysis of these two data structure is the same, we are going to focus on one of them. also note that the running time of updatequery is the sum of that of matrixupdate, partialmatrixupdate, vectorupdate, partialvectorupdate, and query, so we analyze them one by one.

from what we proved in lemma b.28 and lemma b.29, we have  $c_1 = c_4 = \theta(\epsilon)$  and  $c_2 = c_5 = \theta(\epsilon^2)$  and  $c_3 = c_6 = \theta(\epsilon) < 1/4$ .

by theorem c.9 and plugging in  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}} = \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} / 100\log n$ , the expected amortized cost per iteration of the following procedures are as follows:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \text {m a t r i x u p d a t e} = o ^ {*} \left(\left(c _ {1} \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {2 - a / 2} + n ^ {\omega - 1 / 2}\right)\right) \\ = o ^ {*} \left(\left(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {2 - a / 2} + n ^ {\omega - 1 / 2}\right)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \text {p a r t i a l m a t r i x u p d a t e} = o ^ {*} \left(\left(c _ {1} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} + c _ {2} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {1 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2} + n ^ {1 + (\omega - 3 / 2) a}\right)\right) \\ = o ^ {*} \left(\left(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {1 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2} + n ^ {1 + (\omega - 3 / 2) a}\right)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \text {v e c t o r u p d a t e} = o ^ {*} \left(\left(c _ {4} \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot n ^ {1. 5}\right) \\ = o ^ {*} \left(\left(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot n ^ {1. 5}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \text {p a r t i a l v e c t o r u p d a t e} = o ^ {*} \left(\left(c _ {4} \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2} + c _ {5} / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {f a r}} ^ {2}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {1. 5} + n ^ {2 a - \widetilde {a} / 2}\right)\right) \\ = o ^ {*} \left(\left(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {1. 5} + n ^ {2 a - \widetilde {a} / 2}\right)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {q u e r y} = o ^ {*} \left(\mathcal {t} _ {\mathrm {m a t}} \left(n ^ {\widetilde {a}}, n ^ {a}, n ^ {\widetilde {a}}\right) + n ^ {1 + b}\right) \\ = o ^ {*} \left(n ^ {(\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a} + n ^ {1 + b}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

so the overall expected amortized cost of one iteration is

$$
\begin{array}{l} m a t r i x u p d a t e + p a r t i a l m a t r i x u p d a t e \\ + \text {v e c t o r u p d a t e} + \text {p a r t i a l v e c t o r u p d a t e} + \text {q u e r y} \\ = o ^ {*} \left(\underbrace {\left(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {2 - a / 2} + n ^ {\omega - 1 / 2}\right)} _ {\text {m a t r i x u p d a t e}} + \underbrace {\left(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {1 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2} + n ^ {1 + (\omega - 3 / 2) a}\right)} _ {\text {p a r t i a l m a t r i x u p d a t e}}\right) \\ + \underbrace {(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}) \cdot n ^ {1 . 5}} _ {\text {v e c t o r u p d a t e}} + \underbrace {(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}) \cdot (n ^ {1 . 5} + n ^ {2 a - \widetilde {a} / 2})} _ {\text {p a r t i a l v e c t o r u p d a t e}} + \underbrace {n ^ {(\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a} + n ^ {1 + b}} _ {\text {q u e r y}}) \\ = o ^ {*} \big ((\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}) \cdot (n ^ {\omega - 1 / 2} + n ^ {2 - a / 2} + n ^ {1 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2}) + n ^ {(\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a} + n ^ {1 + b} \big), \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last step we use  $\omega \geq 2$ ,  $\widetilde{a} \leq a \leq 1$  and  $\omega - 1/2 = 1 + (\omega - 3/2) \geq 1 + (\omega - 3/2)a$ .

now we are ready to prove the main theorem of this paper.

theorem g.3 (restate theorem 4.1, main result, third improvement). given a linear program  $\min_{ax = b,x\geq 0}c^{\top}x$  with no redundant constraints. assume that the polytope has diameter  $r$  in  $\ell_1$  norm, namely, for any  $x\geq 0$  with  $ax = b$ , we have  $\| x\| _1\leq r$ .

then, for any  $\delta \in (0,1]$ ,  $\mathrm{main}(a,b,c,\delta)$  (algorithm 17) outputs  $x \geq 0$  such that

$$
c ^ {\top} x \leq \min  _ {a x = b, x \geq 0} c ^ {\top} x + \delta \| c \| _ {\infty} r, \quad a n d \quad \| a x - b \| _ {1} \leq \delta \cdot (r \| a \| _ {1} + \| b \| _ {1})
$$

in expected time

$$
\widetilde {o} \left(n ^ {\omega + o (1)} + n ^ {2. 5 - a / 2 + o (1)} + n ^ {1. 5 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2 + o (1)} + n ^ {0. 5 + a + (\omega - 1) \widetilde {a}}\right) \cdot \log (n / \delta)
$$

where  $\omega$  is the exponent of matrix multiplication,  $\alpha$  is the dual exponent of matrix multiplication, and  $0 < a \leq \alpha$ .

in the ideal case when  $\omega = 2$  and  $\alpha = 1$ . the running time is  $\widetilde{o}(n^{2 + 1/18})$ . for general  $2 \leq \omega \leq 3$  and  $0 \leq \alpha \leq 1$ , the running time is  $o^*(n^\omega + n^{2.5 - a/2} + n^{(8 + \sqrt{19})/6}) = o^*(n^\omega + n^{2.5 - a/2} + n^{2.06})$ .

proof. we use the parameters of table 15 to prove the theorem. since  $t$  is decreasing by a  $(1 - \frac{\epsilon}{3\sqrt{n}})$  factor, the main algorithm will take  $o(\epsilon^{-1}\sqrt{n}\log (n / \delta))$  iterations in total.

thus the total running time is

#iterations  $\cdot$  cost per iteration

$$
\begin{array}{l} = o \left(\epsilon^ {- 1} \sqrt {n} \log (n / \delta)\right) \cdot o ^ {*} \left(\left(\epsilon / \epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}}\right) \cdot \left(n ^ {\omega - 1 / 2} + n ^ {2 - a / 2} + n ^ {1 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2}\right) + n ^ {(\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a} + n ^ {1 + b}\right) \\ = o ^ {*} \big (\epsilon_ {\mathrm {m p}} ^ {- 1} (n ^ {\omega} + n ^ {2. 5 - a / 2} + n ^ {1. 5 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2}) + \epsilon^ {- 1} (n ^ {0. 5 + (\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a} + n ^ {1. 5 + b}) \big) \cdot \log (n / \delta). \\ \end{array}
$$

by plugging in the parameters  $\epsilon = o(1 / \log n)$ ,  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} = o(1 / \log n)$ , and  $b = \sqrt{n} \log^{10} n$  (see table 15), the above running time becomes

$$
o ^ {*} \left(n ^ {\omega} + n ^ {2. 5 - a / 2} + n ^ {1. 5 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2} + n ^ {0. 5 + (\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a}\right) \cdot \log (n / \delta), \tag {69}
$$

and recall that parameters  $a$  and  $\widetilde{a}$  need to satisfy that  $a\leq \alpha$  and  $\widetilde{a}\leq \alpha a$

therefore, in the ideal case where  $\omega = 2$  and  $\alpha = 1$ , we can choose  $a = \frac{8}{9}$  and  $\widetilde{a} = \frac{2}{3}$ , and we have  $2.5 - a / 2 = 1.5 + a - \widetilde{a} / 2 = 0.5 + (\omega - 1)\widetilde{a} + a = 2 + 1 / 18$ , so the above running time simplifies to

$$
o ^ {*} (n ^ {2 + 1 / 1 8 + o (1)}) \cdot \log (n / \delta).
$$

for general  $\omega$  and  $\alpha$ , the parameters are optimized as follows:

$$
a = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \alpha , & \text {i f} \alpha \leq \frac {4 w}{3 (2 w - 1)}, \\ \frac {4 w}{3 (2 w - 1)}, & \text {o . w .} \end{array} \right. \quad \widetilde {a} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \min  \{\alpha^ {2}, \frac {2}{2 \omega - 1} \}, & \text {i f} \alpha \leq \frac {4 \omega}{3 (2 \omega - 1)}, \\ \frac {2}{2 \omega - 1}, & \text {o . w .} \end{array} \right.
$$

here we prove the final running time by discussing two cases.

1. in the first case where  $\alpha \leq \frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega - 1)}$ , we have  $a = \alpha$  and  $\widetilde{a} \leq \alpha^2 = \alpha a$ .

if  $\widetilde{a} = \alpha^2$ , then  $1.5 + \alpha - \widetilde{a}/2 = 1.5 + \alpha - \alpha^2/2 \leq 2.5 - \alpha/2$  since  $\alpha \leq 1$ .

if  $\widetilde{a} = \frac{2}{2\omega - 1}$ , then  $1.5 + \alpha - \widetilde{a}/2 = 1.5 + \alpha - 1/(2\omega - 1) \leq 2.5 - \alpha/2$ , since  $\alpha \leq \frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega - 1)}$  and  $\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega - 1)}$  is the value that balances the two terms. thus the following inequality holds:

$$
n ^ {1. 5 + a - \tilde {a} / 2} \leq n ^ {2. 5 - a / 2}.
$$

we also have  $0.5 + (\omega - 1)\widetilde{a} + a \leq 1.5 + a - \widetilde{a}/2$ , since  $\widetilde{a} \leq \frac{2}{2\omega - 1}$  and  $\frac{2}{2\omega - 1}$  is the value that balances these two terms. thus the following inequality also holds:

$$
n ^ {0. 5 + (\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a} \leq n ^ {1. 5 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2}.
$$

therefore the running time of eq. (69) is dominated by  $o(n^{\omega} + n^{2.5 - \alpha / 2})$  in the first case.

2. in the second case where  $\alpha >\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega - 1)}$  , we have  $a = \frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega - 1)}\leq \alpha$  , and  $\widetilde{a} = \frac{2}{2\omega - 1}\leq (\frac{4\omega}{3(2\omega - 1)})^2\leq$ $\alpha a$  , where the second step follows since  $\omega \geq 2$  . with these parameters, we have that

$$
2. 5 - a / 2 = 1. 5 + a - \widetilde {a} / 2 = 0. 5 + (\omega - 1) \widetilde {a} + a = \frac {1 3}{6} - \frac {1}{3 (2 \omega - 1)}.
$$

therefore in the second case the running time of eq. (69) is dominated by

$$
o \left(n ^ {\omega} + n ^ {\frac {1 3}{6} - \frac {1}{3 (2 \omega - 1)}}\right) \leq o \left(n ^ {\omega} + n ^ {(8 + \sqrt {1 9}) / 6}\right),
$$

where  $(8 + \sqrt{19}) / 6\approx 2.0598\leq 2.06$  is the solution of equation  $\omega = \frac{13}{6} -\frac{1}{3(2\omega - 1)}$

thus the running time in eq. (69) is always upper bounded by

$$
o ^ {*} \big (n ^ {\omega} + n ^ {2. 5 - \alpha / 2} + n ^ {(8 + \sqrt {1 9}) / 6} \big) \cdot \log (n / \delta).
$$

# h multi-level with more details

in this section we provide more details for section 2.

# h.1 lu-decomposition of woodbury identity when  $k = 3$

the lu-decomposition of matrix  $d = \left[ \begin{array}{ccc}m & u_2 & u_3\\ v_2^\top & -c_2^{-1} & 0\\ v_3^\top & 0 & -c_3^{-1} \end{array} \right]$  where the diagonal blocks of  $l$  are identity matrices is

$$
\begin{array}{l} d = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} i & 0 & 0 \\ v _ {2} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} & i & 0 \\ v _ {3} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} & 0 & i \end{array} \right] \cdot \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} m & u _ {2} & u _ {3} \\ 0 & - c _ {2} ^ {- 1} - v _ {2} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {2} & - v _ {2} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {3} \\ 0 & - v _ {3} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {2} & - c _ {3} ^ {- 1} - v _ {3} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {3} \end{array} \right] \\ = \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{c c c} i & 0 & 0 \\ v _ {2} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} & i & 0 \\ v _ {3} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} & 0 & i \end{array} \right] \cdot \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} i & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & i & 0 \\ 0 & - v _ {3} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {2} b ^ {- 1} & i \end{array} \right]} _ {l} \\ \cdot \underbrace {\left[ \begin{array}{l l l} m & u _ {2} & u _ {3} \\ 0 & b & - v _ {2} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {3} \\ 0 & 0 & - c _ {3} ^ {- 1} - v _ {3} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {3} - v _ {3} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {2} b ^ {- 1} v _ {2} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {3} \end{array} \right]} _ {u}, \tag {70} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $b\coloneqq -c_2^{-1} - v_2^\top m^{-1}u_2$

# h.2 online low-rank inverse and the omv conjecture.

the following static data structure problem has received significant attention recently (see [hkns15, bns19] and references therein).

definition h.1 (online matrix-vector multiplication (omv)). preprocess a (fixed) matrix  $m \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  so that, given an online sequence of  $t$  vectors  $h_t \in \mathbb{r}^n$  (arriving one by one), the data structure can efficiently output  $mh_t$  (exactly) before the next iteration  $t + 1$ .

the omv conjecture [hkns15] states that with  $\mathrm{poly}(n)$  preprocessing time, the (amortized) query time of any word-ram data structure for omv is at least  $t_q > n^{2 - o(1)}$ . note that this is in sharp contrast to the offline setting where the vectors  $\{h_t\}_{t\in t}$  are given as a batch, in which case fast-matrix multiplication can achieve  $n^{\omega -1} < n^{1.37}$  query time on average (assuming  $t = n$ , say). now consider the following static problem:

definition h.2 (online low-rank inverse multiplication). preprocess a fixed matrix  $m \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  and a fixed vector  $h$ , so that given an online sequence of  $t$  pairs of vectors  $u_{t}, v_{t} \in \mathbb{r}^{n}$ , the data structure can efficiently output  $(m + u_{t}v_{t}^{\top})^{-1}h$  (exactly) before the next iteration  $t + 1$ .

perhaps surprisingly, we prove that these problems are essentially equivalent in the word-ram model with polynomial preprocessing time. we first show that omv is at least as hard as the problem in definition h.2:

lemma h.3. if there is a data structure  $a$  with polynomial preprocessing time for omv (definition h.1) with worst case query time  $\mathcal{t}_a(n,t)$ , then there is a data structure  $a'$  for the online low-rank inverse problem in definition h.2 with polynomial preprocessing time and  $o(\mathcal{t}_a(n,t))$  query time.

proof. we design  $a'$  as follows. in the preprocessing time, we use  $o(n^{\omega})$  time to pre-compute the vector  $x \coloneqq m^{-1} \cdot h \in \mathbb{r}^n$  and run the omv data structure  $a$  on the input matrix  $m^{-1}$ . recall in the query stage, we are given  $u_t, v_t \in \mathbb{r}^n$ . by woodbury's identity, the solution  $(m + u_t v_t^\top)^{-1} \cdot h$  can be written as

$$
(m + u _ {t} v _ {t} ^ {\top}) ^ {- 1} h = m ^ {- 1} h - m ^ {- 1} u _ {t} \left(1 + v _ {t} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {t}\right) ^ {- 1} v _ {t} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} \cdot h.
$$

thus, using a single invocation of the query algorithm of  $a$ , we can compute the product  $y := m^{-1} \cdot u_t$ , and the remaining calculation is

$$
m ^ {- 1} h - m ^ {- 1} u _ {t} \left(1 + v _ {t} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} u _ {t}\right) ^ {- 1} v _ {t} ^ {\top} m ^ {- 1} \cdot h = x - y \left(1 + v _ {t} ^ {\top} \cdot y\right) ^ {- 1} v _ {t} ^ {\top} \cdot x,
$$

which only involves vector inner product calculation and can be done in  $o(n)$  time.

therefore,  $a'$  takes  $o(\mathcal{t}_a(n,t)) + o(tn)$  worst case query time. the lemma is proved by observing that  $\mathcal{t}_a(n,t)$  is at least  $\omega(tn)$ .

we proceed to the other direction of the proof.

lemma h.4. given a word-ram data structure  $b$  with polynomial preprocessing time for the online low-rank inverse problem, with worst-case query time  $\mathcal{t}_b(n,t)$ , there is a data structure  $b'$  for the omv problem with polynomial preprocessing time and  $o(\mathcal{t}_b(n,t))$  worst case query time.

proof. we construct  $b'$  as follows: given the input  $m$  in definition h.1, the data structure first computes  $m^{-1}$ , and finds an arbitrary vector  $h$  for which  $h^{\top}mh \neq 0$ , then precomputes  $x := mh$  and  $y := h^{\top}m$ . this takes  $o(n^{\omega})$  preprocessing time. we can now invoke the preprocessing function of the data structure  $b$  (for online low-rank inverse) with inputs  $m \gets m^{-1}$ ,  $h \gets h$ . in each iteration  $t \in [t]$ , given the vector  $h_t$  of definition h.1, invoke  $b$  with query vector  $u_t \gets h_t$ ,  $v_t \gets h$  to get an answer  $g$ . note that by woodbury's identity

$$
g = (m ^ {- 1} + u _ {t} v _ {t} ^ {\top}) ^ {- 1} h = m h - m u _ {t} (1 + v _ {t} ^ {\top} m u _ {t}) ^ {- 1} v _ {t} ^ {\top} m \cdot h = m h - m h _ {t} (1 + h ^ {\top} m u _ {t}) ^ {- 1} h ^ {\top} m \cdot h.
$$

then  $b^{\prime}$  outputs the query answer

$$
(x - g) \cdot \frac {1 + y \cdot h _ {t}}{y \cdot h} = (m h - g) \cdot \frac {1 + (h ^ {\top} m) h _ {t}}{h ^ {\top} m \cdot h} = m h _ {t},
$$

which only involves vector inner product calculation and can be done in  $o(n)$  time.

therefore,  $b'$  takes polynomial preprocessing time and  $o(\mathcal{t}_b(n,t)) + o(tn)$  worst case query time. the lemma is proved by observing that  $\mathcal{t}_b(n,t)$  is at least  $\omega (tn)$ .

as a corollary, we get that the following problem is at least as hard as the omv problem:

definition h.5 (online cumulative low-rank inverse). preprocess a fixed matrix  $m \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times n}$  and a fixed vector  $h$ , so that given an online sequence of  $t$  pairs of vectors  $u_{t}, v_{t} \in \mathbb{r}^{n}$ , the data structure can efficiently output  $(m + \sum_{i=1}^{t} u_{i} v_{i}^{\top})^{-1} h$  (exactly) before the next iteration  $t + 1$ .

# h.3 optimizing the parameters of eq. (3)

claim h.6. for any positive integer  $n$ ,  $k$ , the following equation holds.

$$
\min _ {n = n _ {1} \geq n _ {2} \geq \dots n _ {k - 1} \geq n _ {k} \geq 1} \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k - 1} n \cdot n _ {k} / \sqrt {n _ {k + 1}} + n _ {k - 1} \cdot n _ {k}\right) = o (k) \cdot n ^ {1. 5 + \frac {1}{6 \cdot (2 ^ {k - 1} - 1)}}.
$$

proof. denote  $p = \frac{1}{6 \cdot (2^{k-1}-1)}$ . define  $a_k$  as the exponent of  $n_k$  such that  $n_k = n^{a_k}$ . then it is equivalent to finding  $a_1, \dots, a_k \in \mathbb{r}$  such that the following three conditions hold:

1.  $1 = a_{1} \geq a_{2} \geq \dots \geq a_{k - 1} \geq a_{k} \geq 0$ .  
2.  $1 + a_{i} - a_{i + 1} / 2\leq 1.5 + p,\forall i\in [k - 1].$  
3.  $a_{k - 1} + a_k\leq 1.5 + p$

the optimal solution is given by  $a_{i} = 1 - 1 / (3\cdot 2^{k - i}) + (2 - 2^{-k + i + 1})\cdot p, \forall i\in [k]$ . now we prove that the three conditions are satisfied with this solution.

condition 1.

$$
a _ {1} = 1 - \frac {1}{3 \cdot 2 ^ {k - 1}} + \frac {2 - 2 ^ {- k + 2}}{6 \cdot (2 ^ {k - 1} - 1)} = 1 - \frac {1}{3 \cdot 2 ^ {k - 1}} + \frac {(2 ^ {k - 1} - 1) / 2 ^ {k - 1}}{3 \cdot (2 ^ {k - 1} - 1)} = 1.
$$

since  $a_i$  is decreasing with  $i$ ,  $a_i \geq a_{i+1}$  is also satisfied. finally,  $a_k = \frac{2}{3} \geq 0$ .

condition 2. for all  $i \in [k - 1]$ ,

$$
\begin{array}{l} 1 + a _ {i} - a _ {i + 1} / 2 = 1 + (1 - \frac {1}{3 \cdot 2 ^ {k - i}} + (2 - 2 ^ {- k + i + 1}) \cdot p) - (1 - \frac {1}{3 \cdot 2 ^ {k - i - 1}} + (2 - 2 ^ {- k + i + 2}) \cdot p) / 2 \\ = 1. 5 - \frac {1}{3 . 2 ^ {k - i}} + (2 - 2 ^ {- k + i + 1}) \cdot p + \frac {1}{3 . 2 ^ {k - i}} - (1 - 2 ^ {- k + i + 1}) \cdot p \\ = 1. 5 + p. \\ \end{array}
$$

condition 3.  $a_{k - 1} + a_k = (1 - \frac{1}{6} +p) + \frac{2}{3} = 1.5 + p.$

# i a feasible algorithm

in previous sections, we show a fast algorithm calculating an lp solution  $\overline{x}$ . however,  $\overline{x}$  is not always a feasible solution since we used sketching to calculate  $\widehat{\delta}_x$  and hence  $a\widehat{\delta}_x$  is not 0. in this section we present how to turn the output  $x$  of theorem 4.1 to a feasible lp solution, i.e.  $\| ax - b\| _1$  is bounded. our technique is based on the robust central path of [lsz19], and we extend it to two level update setting. our algorithm uses  $g,m,u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}$  to implicitly maintain the solutions  $x = g u_{1} + u_{2}$  and  $s = m u_{3} + u_{4}$ . each iteration, the algorithm updates  $g,m,u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}$  so that  $x$  and  $s$  change by  $\widetilde{\delta}_x$  and  $\widetilde{\delta}_s$  in each iteration (see definition b.4). in this way, we can postpone the expensive matrix vector multiplication to every  $\sqrt{n}$  iterations. the running time of maintaining  $x$  and  $s$  is dominated by the pre-existing computations, so our algorithm still achieves the same overall running time. also since we do not multiply the sketching matrix on the left when computing  $\widetilde{\delta}_x$  and  $\widetilde{\delta}_s$ ,  $a\widetilde{\delta}_x = 0$  is always satisfied and in each iteration we always have  $ax = ax^{(0)} = b$ .

we introduce a smaller error constant  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}$  in this section.

definition i.1. we define  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}} = \frac{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} \cdot \epsilon}{3200 \log^3 n}$ .

algorithm 18 feasible version of main (algorithm 17)  
1: procedure main(a,b,c,δ,a, a) ▷ theorem g.3+i.2, a,b,c are inputs of lp, δ is the accuracy parameter  
2:  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} \gets 10^{-5} / \log n$   
3:  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{far}} \gets \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}} / 100 \log n$   
4:  $\epsilon \gets 10^{-7} / \log n$   
5:  $\lambda \gets 40 \log n$   
6:  $\delta \gets \min \{\frac{\delta}{2}, \frac{1}{\lambda}\}$   
7:  $b_{\mathrm{sketch}} \gets 10^{12} \sqrt{n} \log^8 n / \epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}^2$   
8:  $l_{\mathrm{sketch}} \gets n^{1/2 + o(1)}$   
9: create  $l_{\mathrm{sketch}}$  sketching matrices  $r_1, r_2, \dots, r_{l_{\mathrm{sketch}}} \in \mathbb{r}^{b_{\mathrm{sketch}} \times n}$   
10: let  $r = [r_1^\top, r_2^\top, \dots, r_{l_{\mathrm{sketch}}}^\top]^\top$   
11: modify the linear program and obtain an initial  $x^{(0)}$  and  $s^{(0)}$ .  
12: let  $\mathrm{mp}_t$  and  $\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}$  be projection maintenance data structures.  
13: let  $f_t : x \mapsto \sqrt{x}$ ,  $f_{\phi} : x \mapsto \lambda \sinh(\lambda(x - 1)) / \sqrt{x}$   
14:  $\mathrm{mp}_t$ . initializ(ef,  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}, \epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}, a, \widetilde{a}, b_{\mathrm{sketch}}, l_{\mathrm{sketch}}, a, \frac{x^{(0)}}{s^{(0)}}, x^{(0)} s^{(0)}, r)$   
15:  $\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}$ . initializ(ef,  $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}, \epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}, a, \widetilde{a}, b_{\mathrm{sketch}}, l_{\mathrm{sketch}}, a, \frac{x^{(0)}}{s^{(0)}}, \frac{x^{(0)} s^{(0)}}{t}, r)$   
16: global  $\overline{x}, \overline{s}, x, s, t^{new}, w^{old}$   
17:  $\overline{x} \gets x \leftarrow x^{(0)}, \overline{s} \gets s \leftarrow s^{(0)}, w^{old} \leftarrow x^{(0)} s^{(0)}$   
18:  $t^{\mathrm{old}} \gets t \leftarrow 1, j \gets 0$   
19: while  $t > \delta^2 / (32n^3)$  do  
20:  $t^{\mathrm{new}} \gets (1 - \frac{\epsilon}{3\sqrt{n}}) t, j \gets j + 1$   
21: repeat  
22:  $\widehat{\delta}_x, \widehat{\delta}_s, w^{\mathrm{appr}} \gets \text{onestepcentralpath}(\mathrm{mp}_t, \mathrm{mp}_\phi, t, t^{\mathrm{new}})$   
23: if the  $l_{\mathrm{sketch}}$  sketching matrices are used up then  
24: re-initialize  $\mathrm{mp}_t$  and  $\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}$  with new ones.  
25: end if  
26: until  $||\overline{x}^{-1} \widehat{\delta}_x||_{\infty} \leq 5\epsilon, ||\overline{s}^{-1} \widehat{\delta}_s||_{\infty} \leq 5\epsilon,$  if this condition is false, revoke the updates of  $u_1, u_2, u_3, u_4$  in  $\mathrm{mp}_t$  and  $\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}$   
27:  $\overline{x} \gets \overline{x} + \widehat{\delta}_x, \overline{s} \gets \overline{s} + \widehat{\delta}_s$   
28: makefeasible(w^{\mathrm{appr}})  
29: if  $j > \sqrt{n}$  or  $t < t^{\mathrm{old}} / 2$  then  
30:  $x \gets x - (\mathrm{mp}_t u_1 + \mathrm{mp}_t.g \cdot \mathrm{mp}_t.u_2) - (\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.u_1 + \mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.g \cdot \mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.u_2)$   
31:  $s \gets s + (\mathrm{mp}_t.u_3 + \mathrm{mp}_t.m \cdot \mathrm{mp}_t.u_4) + (\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.u_3 + \mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.m \cdot \mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.u_4)$   
32: mp_t. initializ( $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}, \epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}, a, \widetilde{a}, b, l, a, w^{\mathrm{appr}}, h^{\mathrm{appr}}, r)$   
33: mp_t. initializ( $\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}, \epsilon_{\mathrm{far}}, a, \widetilde{a}, b, l, a, w^{\mathrm{appr}}, h^{\mathrm{appr}}, t, r)$   
34:  $j \gets 1, t^{\mathrm{old}} \gets t$   
35: end if  
36: if φ_λ(x̅)/t-1 > n³ then  
37: (\overline{x}, \overline{s}) ← classicalstep(x̅, s̅, tⁿ⁻¹)  
38: x̅ ← x̅, s̅ ← s̅  
39: construct sketching matrices r similar as before.  
40: mp_t. initializ(f_t, ε_m, ε_far, a, a̅, b_s_ketchup, l_s_ketchup, a, x̅/s̅, xs̅/s̅, r)  
41: mp_t. initializ(f_t, ε_m, ε_far, a, a̅, b_s_ketchup, l_s_ketchup, a, x̅/s̅, xs̅/t̅, r)  
42: end if  
43: t ← tⁿ⁻¹  
44: end while  
45: return x  
46: end procedure

algorithm 19 feasible version of onestepcentralpath (algorithm 3)  
1: procedure onestepcentralpath  $(\mathrm{mp}_t,\mathrm{mp}_{\phi},t,t^{\mathrm{new}})$  ▷ part 1 of theorem i.2  
2:  $\overline{w}\gets \overline{x} /\overline{s}$  ▷  $\overline{x},\overline{s}$  is global variable  
3:  $\overline{\mu}\gets \overline{x}\overline{s}$   
4:  $(q_{t,x},p_{t,x},p_{t,s},w^{\mathrm{app}})\gets \mathrm{mp}_t.\mathrm{updatequery}(\overline{w},\overline{\mu})$  ▷ algorithm 23  
5: ▷ this data-structure works with function  $f_{t}(x) = \sqrt{x}$   
6:  $(q_{\phi ,x},p_{\phi ,x},p_{\phi ,s},w^{\mathrm{app}})\gets \mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.\mathrm{updatequery}(\overline{w},\overline{\mu} /t)$  ▷ algorithm 23  
7: ▷ this data-structure works with function  $f_{\phi}(x) = \nabla \phi (x - 1) / \sqrt{x}$   
8:  $\widehat{\delta}_x\gets q_{t,x} + q_{\phi ,x} - (p_{t,x} + p_{\phi ,x})$   
9:  $\widehat{\delta}_s\gets p_{t,s} + p_{\phi ,s}$   
10:  $x\gets x + q_{t,x} + q_{\phi ,x}$   
11: return  $(\widehat{\delta}_x,\widehat{\delta}_s,w^{\mathrm{app}})$   
12: end procedure

algorithm 20 data structure : makefeasible.  
1: data structure  
2: procedure makefeasible  $(w^{\mathrm{app}})$  ▷ part 2 of theorem i.2  
3:  $\widehat{s} \gets \{i : |w_i^{\mathrm{old}} - w_i^{\mathrm{app}}| > w_i^{\mathrm{old}} / 2\}$   
4:  $\overline{x}_{\widehat{s}} \gets x_{\widehat{s}} - ((\mathrm{mp}_t.u_1)_{\widehat{s}} + (\mathrm{mp}_t.g \cdot \mathrm{mp}_t.u_2)_{\widehat{s}}) - ((\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.u_1)_{\widehat{s}} + (\mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.g \cdot \mathrm{mp}_{\phi}.u_2)_{\widehat{s}})$

7: end procedure

section b are still satisfied for the modified algorithm.

robustness of central path. we first prove the following two statements about the robustness of central path method:

1. the  $w^{\mathrm{app}}$  and  $h^{\mathrm{app}}$  used in the data structures satisfy  $w^{\mathrm{app}} \approx_{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} w$ , and  $h^{\mathrm{app}} \approx_{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} \mu$ . (this corresponds to part 1 and 2 of assumption b.5. we only lose a constant factor here.)  
2.  $\mu^{\mathrm{new}}\approx_{0.2}t$  (this corresponds to part 3 of assumption b.5. we only lose a constant factor here.)

in part 3 of theorem i.2, we prove that in any iteration,  $x, \overline{x}$ ,  $s, \overline{s}$  are entry-wise close with high probability, i.e.  $\overline{x} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}} x$  and  $\overline{s} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}} s$  holds with probability  $1 - 1 / \mathrm{poly}(n)$ . we will use this to prove that the above two statements are still satisfied in our modified algorithm.

1. the data structure directly ensures  $w^{\mathrm{appr}} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} \overline{w}$ , and  $h^{\mathrm{appr}} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} \overline{\mu}$ . and since  $\overline{x}^{(j)} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}} x^{(j)}$  and  $\overline{s}^{(j)} \approx_{\epsilon_{\mathrm{tiny}}} s^{(j)}$  (part 3 of theorem i.2) we directly get the desired result that  $w^{\mathrm{appr}} \approx_{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} w$  and  $h^{\mathrm{appr}} \approx_{2\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}}} \mu$ .

2. note that previously  $\mu \approx_{0.1} t$  was proved in lemma b.27 by bounding the potential function. and the proof of lemma b.27 uses the bounds given by lemma b.21. we define the potential function to be  $\phi\left(\frac{xs}{t}-1\right)$  here instead of  $\phi\left(\frac{\overline{x}s}{t}-1\right)$ . note that conditioned on  $\overline{x}^{(j)}$  and  $\overline{s}^{(j)}$ ,  $x^{(j+1)}$  and  $s^{(j+1)}$  are deterministic. thus part 2 and part 4 of lemma b.21 are trivial. we still have an analog of part 1 and part 3 of lemma b.21:  $\|\overline{\mu}^{-1}(\mu^{\mathrm{new}} - \overline{\mu} - \overline{\delta}_t - \widetilde{\delta}_{\phi})\|_2 \leq o(\epsilon_{\mathrm{mp}})$  and  $\|\overline{\mu}^{-1}(\mu^{\mathrm{new}} - \overline{\mu})\|_{\infty} \leq o(\epsilon)$  using the fact that  $\overline{x}^{(j)}$  and  $\overline{s}^{(j)}$  are close to  $x^{(j)}$  and  $s^{(j)}$  (part 3 of theorem i.2). thus we still have an analog of lemma b.27 that

$$
\phi_ {\lambda} (\mu^ {\mathrm {n e w}} / t ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - 1) \leq \phi_ {\lambda} (\overline {{\mu}} / t - 1) - \frac {\lambda \epsilon}{1 5 \sqrt {n}} \cdot (\phi_ {\lambda} (\overline {{\mu}} / t - 1) - 1 0 n).
$$

using part 3 of theorem i.2 again and the fact that the derivative of  $\cosh (x)$  function is constant when  $x < 2$ , we have

$$
\phi_ {\lambda} (\mu^ {\mathrm {n e w}} / t ^ {\mathrm {n e w}} - 1) \leq \phi_ {\lambda} (\mu / t - 1) - \omega (\frac {\lambda \epsilon}{\sqrt {n}}) \cdot (\phi_ {\lambda} (\mu / t - 1) - o (n ^ {2})).
$$

thus we can still inductively prove a polynomial upper bound on the potential function  $\phi_{\lambda}(\frac{xs}{t} -1)$ . note that we only lose a constant factor in the approximation ratio of  $\mu$  with  $t$  when the last term is  $o(n^{2})$  instead of  $o(n)$ .

$w$  and  $h$  move slowly. the last part of the inductive analysis is to show that  $w$  and  $\mu$  are both moving slowly as that of section b.6 and b.7. now we only have the guarantee for  $w$  and  $\mu$ , instead of  $\overline{w}$  and  $\overline{\mu}$ , so we use  $\psi(w_i / v_i - 1)$  and  $\psi(w_i / \widetilde{v}_i - 1)$  in the analysis and use  $\psi(\overline{w}_i / v_i - 1)$  and  $\psi(\overline{w}_i / \widetilde{v}_i - 1)$  for the algorithm (because the algorithm doesn't know  $w$  and  $\mu$ ). the amortized analysis of section f need to be modified accordingly. we have the following two statements:
